\section*{S0. Notation and Pipeline Contract}

\subsection*{S0.2 Per-Region Convention}
Every scaffold, complex, count table, mass field, pressure field, and Gaussian summary is owned by a recursion-tree region $C$. Unless a section explicitly discusses cross-level or cross-region behavior, it is written in the local convention: fix a region $C$ and omit the superscript $(C)$ from all objects. When comparing parent/child regions, sibling regions, or multiple query levels, superscripts are restored.

\subsection*{S0.2.1 Superscript Discipline}
The superscript convention distinguishes single-region statements from statements over the recursion tree.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item Unsuperscripted symbols such as $m_S$, $p_f$, $\mathcal K$, and $\tau^\star$ refer to the single region $C$ fixed by the surrounding section.
  \item Superscripted symbols such as $m_S^{(C)}$, $p_f^{(C)}$, $\mathcal K_C$, and $\tau_C^\star$ appear when a statement ranges over multiple regions in the recursion tree.
  \item $C_\ell$ denotes the $\ell$-th region on a query path, while $C'$ denotes a child region produced by recursive decomposition.
\end{itemize}
This convention distinguishes whether a mass, pressure, or membership score belongs to a single complex or to the multiscale tree.

\subsection*{S0.3 The Three Field Types}
Proteus routes information through three fields with different schedules and meanings.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Transition counts} $\{n_{i\to j}\}$ are empirical observations. They are written online by BMU/neighbor routing and read by Affinity Propagation, pruning diagnostics, and the Dirichlet--multinomial evidence gate.
  \item \emph{Simplex masses} $\{m_S\}$ are latent generative variables on an accepted complex. They summarize how probability mass is distributed over cells after topology selection and settling. The runtime evidence gate does not require an iterative MAP estimate of $m$; it scores transition counts directly under a local Dirichlet--multinomial marginal.
  \item \emph{Face pressures} $\{p_f\}$ are interpolation variables on facets. Empirical tallies $\{\hat p_f\}$ are accumulated online, while the conservative field $\{p_f\}$ is reconstructed after topology selection. Pressures are used for density interpolation and diagnostics, not as a second likelihood term for the same edit decision.
\end{enumerate}
The separation is architectural: counts decide structure; masses and pressures make the accepted structure queryable.

\subsection*{S0.4 Propose--Test--Settle Contract}
All structural changes follow the same lifecycle.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Propose:} a local diagnostic identifies a candidate edit. Examples include a variance-cap split, a weak-link prune, a merge, a torsion-aligned split, or a local warp attachment.
  \item \emph{Test:} the affected region is rerouted under the candidate topology and scored by the local evidence criterion $F_{\mathrm{DM}}$. Evidence, not the diagnostic alone, decides whether the edit is accepted.
  \item \emph{Settle:} accepted edits trigger a local re-equilibration window so subsequent proposals are scored against stabilized counts and moments.
\end{enumerate}
This contract is the shared assumption behind the statistical gates (S3), the Stage~2 construction rules (S4), torsion/warp proposals (S5), and the dynamic-convergence arguments (S12).

\subsection*{S0.5 Canonical Definition Sites}
Several objects appear in multiple parts of Proteus. The following list identifies where their canonical definitions are developed.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item $\Phi_C(\tau)$ and $R_i(\tau)$: S2.5. $R_i$ is a scale diagnostic, not a within-cluster density signal.
  \item $\alpha_{0,i}$: S2.7, derived from the BDeu prior and reused by AP preferences and the evidence gate.
  \item $F_{\mathrm{DM}}$: S3.4, with consistency in S3.5.
  \item The flag-complex invariant and transition-count policies: S4.1--S4.4.
  \item Torsion ratio $R_S$, edge-ratio fallback, and warp policies: S5.
  \item Face pressures $p_f$ and simplex-local density: S6.
  \item Dual / face adjacency for the S10.4 evidence-gate connectivity check: S6.6 (proposed; OPEN\_ISSUES~\#43; \texttt{stage2.dual\_flow} + \texttt{GateConfig.apply\_dual\_adjacency}).
  \item Hollow-edge / empty-region evidence $H(i,j)$, $M_{\mathrm{transit}}$, and the proposed \texttt{prefer\_hollow\_edge\_prepass}: S2.6.2 (OPEN\_ISSUES~\#44; theory note \texttt{reference/empty\_region\_evidence\_and\_scale.md}).
  \item Gaussian summaries $(\boldsymbol\mu_C,\Sigma_C)$, closed-form membership $\mu_C(\mathbf x)$, PL membership $\mu_C^{\mathrm{PL}}$, and trajectory $\boldsymbol\mu(\mathbf x)$: S7.
  \item Generative mass field $m$, geometry-derived router $q(j\mid i;m)$, and identifiability: S10.
  \item Lyapunov function and dynamic convergence lemmas: S12.
\end{itemize}
Later sections may restate these objects locally for readability, but the sections listed above contain the full definitions and operational conventions.

\subsection*{S0.6 Noise Floor Decomposition}
\label{sec:si-noise-floor}
Stage~1 operates in a noise-positive domain. The latent density is modeled as
\[
\rho(\mathbf x)=u(\mathbf x)+\sum_{i=1}^m f_i(\mathbf x),
\qquad
u(\mathbf x)\ge u_{\min}>0,
\]
where the strictly positive tissue term $u(\mathbf x)$ is the contrast
reference against which all features are judged. It is useful to split this
floor into
\[
u(\mathbf x)=u_{\mathrm{global}}+u_{\mathrm{local}}(\mathbf x),
\qquad
u_{\mathrm{global}}>0,\quad u_{\mathrm{local}}(\mathbf x)\ge 0.
\]
The global floor is the ambient-space minimum induced by irreducible
measurement stochasticity; it determines whether any structure is detectable at
all. The local floor is the excess tissue specific to a region or subregion:
unresolved sub-structure, local contamination, or transverse blur from nearby
features. Once recursion isolates a region, $u_{\mathrm{local}}$ becomes the
relevant detectability reference.

Operationally, detectability and resolvability are always interpreted relative
to the floor appropriate to the current recursion context. The root region sees
the global floor; a recursion child sees the local floor within its parent
interior. The purpose of Stage~1 local-noise tracking is not to estimate an
arbitrary covariance model everywhere, but to maintain a conservative,
confidence-gated local floor summary sufficient for scale-selection, cluster
detection, and recursion decisions.

\section*{S1. Preprocessing and Optional Dimensionality Reduction}

\subsection*{S1.1 Randomized SVD Preprocessing}
For very high-dimensional inputs, Proteus may begin with an optional randomized SVD projection. The default protocol samples up to $20{,}000$ rows (or all rows if the data are small), runs a randomized SVD with sketch width $k_{\mathrm{target}}\approx 256$ and oversampling $20$, and selects the retained rank by cumulative explained energy and an intrinsic-dimension floor:
\[
r=\max\!\left(r_{0.999},\,2\,\lceil D_{\mathrm{LB}}\rceil\right),
\]
where $r_{0.999}$ is the smallest rank whose cumulative explained variance exceeds $99.9\%$ and $D_{\mathrm{LB}}$ is a Levina--Bickel intrinsic-dimension estimate. The projection is applied as $Z=XP$ with $P=V_{1:r}^\top$. If the implied reduction is below $5\%$ or near-neighbor recall on a held-out subsample drops materially, skip the projection. Optionally append $32$ Johnson--Lindenstrauss vectors to tighten distance preservation. The projection is a computational preconditioner only: all later scale and topology decisions are still made by the Proteus scaffold.

\subsection*{S1.2 Optional Marginal Gaussianization}
For highly skewed or heavy-tailed coordinates, an optional marginal Gaussianization can improve early neighbor search and moment estimates. The transform is applied in the current working representation: after the S1.1 projection if one is used, or on the original feature coordinates if projection is skipped. A coordinate is eligible only if it has enough observed support for a stable empirical CDF and behaves as a continuous scalar rather than a categorical or nearly binary variable. In first implementations, require at least $\max(1000,10d)$ non-missing observations and at least $50$ distinct values; coordinates that fail these checks are left unchanged.

Among eligible coordinates, transform only those whose marginal distribution is meaningfully non-Gaussian, e.g.\ large absolute skew, heavy tails, or an Anderson--Darling / Kolmogorov--Smirnov rejection of normality on a held-out subsample. For each selected coordinate $u_j$, estimate an empirical CDF $\widehat F_j$ on the training split and transform
\[
z_j=\sqrt{2}\,\operatorname{erf}^{-1}\!\left(2\widehat F_j(u_j)-1\right).
\]
The fitted CDF is then frozen and applied to validation/test samples by interpolation with clipped tail probabilities, e.g.\ $\widehat F_j\in[\epsilon,1-\epsilon]$ with $\epsilon=1/(2n_j)$. This makes each selected marginal approximately standard normal on the training sample, but it is still only a marginal transform; it does not claim to linearize the joint manifold.

The transform is kept only if it preserves the geometry Proteus needs. On a held-out subsample, compare nearest-neighbor recall and rank correlation of local distances before and after the transform; reject the transform if recall drops materially (default: more than $5\%$ absolute at the Stage~1 $k$) or if local distance rank correlation falls below $0.95$. Also run lightweight linearity diagnostics on the transformed space (graph-Laplacian curvature energy and distance correlation are good defaults) and require them to improve or remain statistically indistinguishable. If the linearity test passes at $p\ge 0.01$, the expensive global warp fitting can be skipped at this recursion level and the algorithm can proceed directly to the geometric audit. If the transform worsens neighbor preservation, destabilizes the scale-response peaks, or increases Stage~1 stabilization time, it is rolled back for that recursion level.

\subsection*{S1.3 Distance Preservation and Failure Modes}
Preprocessing is skipped if nearest-neighbor preservation deteriorates, if the retained dimension is not materially smaller, or if downstream scale-response diagnostics become less stable. Implementations should log local neighbor recall before/after projection, explained variance, retained rank, and any change in Stage~1 stabilization time. The default posture is conservative: preprocessing is a speed optimization, not part of the statistical guarantee.

\subsection*{S1.4 Per-Recursion Intrinsic-Dimension Projection}
At every parent-to-child recursion boundary, Proteus re-expresses each child region in a local PCA frame before the child's next Stage~1 scale search. This step is mandatory: optional marginal Gaussianization and non-linear warps are both fitted and evaluated after this transfer, in the child PCA frame. The PCA therefore fixes the working coordinates for the recursion level; later transforms add Gaussianization or local non-linear capacity without replacing the inherited intrinsic-dimension projection. The PCA is fitted in the active parent frame, meaning after any accepted parent-level projection or warp has already been applied.

Let $C'$ be a child region produced by AP clustering in parent region $C$, and let $\mathcal V_{C'}$ be the parent scaffold nodes assigned to that child. Define the child intrinsic-dimension summary by the cluster median
\[
\widehat d_{C'}=
\operatorname{median}\{d_{\mathrm{final},i}:i\in\mathcal V_{C'}\}.
\]
The target rank is
\[
r_{\mathrm{ID}}(C')=
\max\!\left(r_{\min},\,2\lceil \widehat d_{C'}\rceil\right),
\]
with default $r_{\min}=3$ for non-degenerate local neighborhoods. Run a PCA or randomized SVD on the centered routed child samples in the active parent frame and compute the usual explained-energy rank $r_{0.999}(C')$. The retained child working rank is
\[
r_{C'}=
\min\!\left(D_C,\,
\max\{r_{\mathrm{ID}}(C'),\,r_{0.999}(C')\}
\right),
\]
where $D_C$ is the parent working dimension. Child samples and inherited node positions are projected into the first $r_{C'}$ principal directions before recursion begins, and the child's $D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$ is set to $r_{C'}$.

The distance-preservation audit of S1.3 is still applied locally. If truncating to $r_{C'}$ materially degrades nearest-neighbor recall or local distance ranks, keep the full child PCA basis in the parent dimension and log the failed truncation; the mandatory operation is the child PCA reparameterization, while rank truncation remains guarded by neighbor preservation. Child-level marginal Gaussianization and all subsequent region or patch warp proposals are then fitted in this PCA frame. This makes those transforms cheaper because their input dimension is $r_{C'}$ rather than the parent dimension, while the explained-energy and neighbor-preservation guards ensure that no accepted child-level information is discarded by the rank choice. Along a root-to-leaf path, projections compose with the parent representation, so each child works inside the span made available by its ancestors and no downstream level invents directions outside the accepted parent frame.

\subsection*{S1.4.1 Per-Node Intrinsic-Dimension Estimator}
\label{sec:si-intrinsic-dim}
The per-node intrinsic dimension $d_{\mathrm{final},i}$ used throughout Stage~1
(the recursion PCA-rank summary of S1.4, the BDeu/DM branching prior
$\alpha_{0,i}=1/(d_{\mathrm{final},i}+1)$ of S2.7, the AP self-preference of
S2.6.1, the link-prune slot count of S3.1, and the junction diagnostics of
S8.4) is, by default, a \emph{degree proxy} on the lifted structural graph.
Writing $\mathcal N(i)$ for the lifted neighbors of node~$i$, the raw estimate
is $\widetilde d_i=\max(1,\,\deg(i)-1)$ and the reported value is the
neighbor-median smoothed integer
\[
d_{\mathrm{final},i}
=\max\!\Big(1,\ \operatorname{round}\ \operatorname{median}
\big\{\widetilde d_j:j\in\{i\}\cup\mathcal N(i)\big\}\Big),
\]
clipped to the ambient dimension. The intuition is that a locally
$d$-dimensional cap-equilibrated cell has on the order of $d+1$ Delaunay
neighbors, so $\deg-1$ tracks $d$.

\paragraph{Validation.}
On the uniform $d$-ball reference ensemble (the same ensemble that calibrates
$c_{d,k}$ and $C_Q(d)$; S2.5.5, S3.3) the degree proxy is \emph{unbiased}: the
per-node median equals the true dimension for $d\in\{1,2,3,4\}$. Its documented
failure mode is an \emph{upward} bias on a thin curved manifold embedded in a
higher ambient dimension: off-manifold lifted edges---the same high-dimensional
Delaunay slivers that the directed-floor link pruning of S3.1--S3.2 is designed
to suppress---inflate node degree. On a $1$-D circle embedded in $\mathbb R^3$
the proxy reports a median of $2$ against a true dimension of $1$. Because the
proxy over-reads only where the neighborhood mixes off-manifold adjacency, and
the junction detector of S8.4 keys on the \emph{range} of $d_{\mathrm{final}}$
across a neighborhood rather than its absolute value, a uniform over-read on a
homogeneous thin manifold does not manufacture spurious junctions; the bias is
conservative for the proposal-path consumers (larger $\alpha_{0,i}$, larger PCA
rank floor) that it feeds.

\paragraph{Levina--Bickel cross-check.}
For absolute-dimension reporting we also provide the maximum-likelihood
estimator of Levina and Bickel. From a point's sorted nearest-neighbor
distances $T_1\le\cdots\le T_k$ (self excluded), the estimate at neighbor count
$k$ is
\[
m_k(i)=\Big[\tfrac{1}{k-1}\textstyle\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}\log\!\big(T_k(i)/T_j(i)\big)\Big]^{-1},
\]
and, following MacKay--Ghahramani, the per-node estimate averages the inverses
$1/m_k(i)$ over $k\in[k_{\min},k_{\max}]$ (defaults $6,12$) before inverting,
then applies the same neighbor-median smoothing. Using geometry directly, this
estimator is not inflated by structural edges: it recovers the true dimension
on well-sampled (locally Poissonian) manifolds---exact on the dense $d$-ball for
$d\in\{1,2,3,4\}$, and $d=1$ on the embedded circle where the proxy over-reads.
It assumes Poisson local sampling, so it \emph{under-reads} on the near-regular
node lattice of an equilibrated coarse scaffold at $d\gtrsim3$; it is therefore
used on the dense routed sample set or as an independent validation estimator,
not as a coarse-node drop-in. The scaffold exposes both via an
\texttt{intrinsic\_dim\_method} selector (\texttt{"degree"} default,
\texttt{"mle"} for Levina--Bickel). Dimension-sensitive acceptance consumers
(junction detection S8.4, heterogeneous simplex dimension S4.2) should prefer
the scale-consistent estimator rather than inherit a silent proxy bias; the
$k_{\min},k_{\max}$ range is an operational default (S14.3).

\paragraph{Operational refresh semantics.}
The two estimators above define the value produced by an \emph{explicit} refresh
of the per-node estimate; they are not evaluated implicitly on every edit. In the
Stage~1 stabilization loop the per-node value is \emph{seeded} to the region
working dimension $D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$ (S1.4) when a node is created and is
inherited unchanged across variance-cap splits; the scaffold does not recompute
it mid-run. The refresh is a diagnostic/offline operation, invoked for junction
diagnostics (S8.4) and for the validation protocol above. Consequently, within a
single uniform-scale scaffold run ($\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}=\tau$ for all $i$,
S2.4) every operational consumer that reads a $d_{\mathrm{final}}$ aggregate ---
the S2.6.1 clustering effective dimension
$d_{\mathrm{eff}}=\operatorname{median}_i d_{\mathrm{final},i}$, the S2.7 branching
prior $\alpha_{0,i}=1/(d_{\mathrm{final},i}+1)$, the AP self-preference (S2.6.1),
the recursion child dimension summary $\hat d=\operatorname{median}$ over cluster
members (S1.4), and the S3.1--S3.2 post-clustering lifted-edge demotion cluster
dimension --- evaluates at the region working dimension $D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$.
This is intended and consistent with the uniform-cap design of S2.4: dimensional
heterogeneity is resolved \emph{across} recursion levels (each child receives its
own PCA rank via T2 transfer and its own scale search), not \emph{within} a single
scaffold run. Consumers that require a genuine per-node estimate departing from
$D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$ --- junction detection (S8.4) and heterogeneous simplex
dimension (S4.2) in Stage~2 --- must invoke the refresh explicitly on the
scale-consistent sample rather than read the seeded value.

\section*{S2. Stage 1 Derivations}

\subsection*{S2.1 Geometric Grid Spacing}
\label{sec:si-grid-spacing}
Lindeberg-normalized scale-space responses obey $\Delta_{\mathrm{FWHM}}(\log\sigma)\approx 0.586$: peaks separated by less than this in log-bandwidth are not reliably resolvable. Choose adjacent grid scales separated by half the FWHM,
\[
\log\sigma_{j+1}-\log\sigma_j
=\Delta\approx\tfrac12\Delta_{\mathrm{FWHM}}
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\frac{\sigma_{j+1}}{\sigma_j}=e^\Delta\approx\sqrt{2}.
\]
Since $\tau=\sigma^2$, the corresponding grid ratio in the variance cap is $\tau_{j+1}/\tau_j\approx 1/2$. In practice we use the in-$\sigma$ ratio $r=1/\sqrt{2}\approx 0.71$ (about 7--9 points per decade) and map consistently into $\tau$. This is the resolution criterion behind the default $r=1/\sqrt{2}$.

The geometric grid is the canonical bookkeeping lattice, not the full search
policy. In the presence of a locally varying floor, Stage~1 should treat the
grid as the set of admissible \emph{candidate} scales and adapt traversal to
the current local floor belief. Coarser scales remain attractive while a region
is still merged into composite structure; finer scales are favored only after
the current scale is both stabilized and locally floor-feasible. In practice,
the adaptive policy is driven by the local floor summaries of
\hyperref[sec:si-local-noise]{S2.5.2--S2.5.3}: the geometric grid stays fixed,
but the controller's confidence in each step becomes floor-aware rather than
globally homoscedastic.

\subsection*{S2.2 EWMA Weight and Neighbor Routing Weights}
\label{sec:si-neighbor-weights}
The EWMA recursion $m_{t+1}=(1-\alpha)m_t+\alpha x_{t+1}$ has half-life $T_{1/2}=\ln(0.5)/\ln(1-\alpha)\approx \ln 2/\alpha$ for small $\alpha$. Setting $T_{1/2}=k$ (one effective neighborhood of updates) yields
\[
\alpha=\frac{\ln 2}{k}.
\]
This ties estimator memory to the effective neighborhood support: changing $k$ scales the half-life by the same factor without altering the bias/variance interpretation of the moments. Equivalently, with effective neighborhood mass $\Heff$, the standard half-window rule $\alpha\approx 2/(\Heff+1)$ is recovered when $\Heff\approx 2k/\ln 2-1$.

\paragraph{Neighbor routing weights.}
Within the $k$-nearest set returned by the ANN query, each neighbor receives a routing weight $\omega_r$ that scales its EWMA moment update, its contribution to the deferred nudge, and its Hebbian tally. Two regimes are used, selected by the working dimension $D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$ of the current region frame:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Low dimension} ($D_{\mathrm{subspace}}\le d_{\mathrm{cut}}$, default $d_{\mathrm{cut}}=8$): \emph{relative Gaussian weights}. With squared distances $\{r_j^2\}$ and nearest squared distance $r_{(0)}^2$,
  \[
  \omega_j=\exp\!\Big(-\frac{r_j^2-r_{(0)}^2}{2\tau}\Big),
  \]
  so the nearest neighbor (BMU) has weight $1$ and the others decay by their squared-distance excess at bandwidth $\tau$. This uses the metric directly, which is reliable when low-dimensional distances are informative.
  \item \emph{High dimension} ($D_{\mathrm{subspace}}>d_{\mathrm{cut}}$): \emph{rank-decay approximation} $\omega_r=2^{-(r-1)}$ for rank $r=1,\dots,k$ (BMU at rank $1$). Neighbor ranks stay stable under the distance concentration that degrades raw Gaussian weights in high dimension, and the geometric decay preserves the same qualitative BMU-dominated shape at lower cost.
\end{itemize}
The cutoff $d_{\mathrm{cut}}$ is an operational default (\texttt{routing\_weights.gaussian\_cutoff\_dim}) trading metric fidelity against high-dimensional distance concentration; it may be retuned per data regime. The rank-decay form is the one written out in the reference algorithms of S13.2--S13.3.

\subsection*{S2.3 Dual-Rate Motion from Variance Correction}
\label{sec:si-dual-rate-motion}
Stage~1 separates fast statistical tracking from slower geometric movement. For sample $\mathbf x$ and node $i$, define residual $\mathbf e_i=\mathbf x-\mathbf w_i$. Moment updates track $\mathbb E[\mathbf e_i]$ and $\mathbb E[\mathbf e_i\mathbf e_i^\top]$ immediately. Geometric motion is driven by the EWMA residual mean $\mathbf m_i$ rather than by raw per-sample residuals: $\mathbf m_i$ estimates persistent local bias, while the deferred nudge variable $\mathbf a_i$ accumulates only scale-resolved displacement before committing it to the node position. Only when $\|\mathbf a_i\|$ exceeds a displacement threshold is $\mathbf w_i$ updated and the ANN index refreshed. This dual-rate design keeps neighbor search stable and allows convergence arguments to reason about virtual positions $\mathbf y_i=\mathbf w_i+\mathbf a_i$ between realized moves.

\paragraph{Node state.}
Each node stores hit count $h_i$, residual mean $\mathbf m_i$, residual second moment $\mathbf s_i$, derived variance $\sigma_i^2$, an Oja direction $\mathbf u_i$ for dominant local variance, directed transition counts $n_{i\to j}$, and deferred nudge $\mathbf a_i$. A variance-cap violation $\sigma_i^2>\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}$ proposes additional local capacity, typically by splitting along $\mathbf u_i$ after evidence and budget checks. Low-hit, low-variance nodes or weak bilateral links become prune candidates. The variance cap is therefore both a geometric resolution target and a proposal generator; the evidence gate decides whether the proposed topology change is retained.

\paragraph{Variance-correction rate (analysis quantity, not an applied update).}
The operational motion law is the deferred-nudge (centering) path only: node positions move through the accumulator $\mathbf a_i$, and no per-update GNG rate is applied in the scaffold loop (S13.1--S13.2). It is nonetheless useful to name the \emph{effective} drift rate that the deferred nudge induces on cap-satisfied intervals, because the convergence analysis of S12 reasons in those terms. Under local isotropy, the node variance satisfies $\sigma_i^2\approx \mathbb E\|x-w_i\|^2$ and a radial shift $\delta r$ changes the variance by $\Delta\sigma_i^2\approx 2\sqrt{\sigma_i^2}\,\delta r$. Spreading this correction over $H=k$ effective updates gives the GNG-equivalent rate
\[
\eta_{\mathrm{GNG},i}
\approx
\frac{\Delta\sigma_i^2}{2H\,\sigma_i^2}
=\frac{1}{2k}\!\left(1-\frac{\sigma_i^2}{\tau}\right),
\quad\text{or, with the half-life convention,}\quad
\eta_{\mathrm{GNG},i}=\frac{\ln 2}{2k}\!\left(1-\frac{\sigma_i^2}{\tau}\right).
\]
This rate is a diagnostic and an analysis handle: it is the effective drift envelope used in S12.1--S12.2, \emph{not} a second update applied alongside the deferred nudge. Its vanishing at the cap ($\sigma_i^2\to\tau$) expresses the same self-regulation the deferred nudge realizes operationally---a cap-saturated node proposes a split rather than continuing to move. The reference implementation exposes $\eta_{\mathrm{GNG}}$ as a derived quantity (\texttt{rates.eta\_gng}) for this analysis role only.

\paragraph{Centering rate.}
The deferred nudge fires when $\|\mathbf a_i\|$ exceeds the resolution threshold
\[
\delta_{\min}=\kappa(1-r)\sqrt{\tau},
\qquad \kappa=0.5\ \text{(default)},
\]
where $r=1/\sqrt{2}$ is the grid ratio of S2.1. Under expected drift per update of order $\sqrt{\tau}$, the centering rate that produces one realized move per $k$ updates satisfies $k\,\eta_{\mathrm{cent}}\,\sqrt{\tau}=\delta_{\min}$, hence
\[
\eta_{\mathrm{cent}}=\frac{\kappa(1-r)}{k}.
\]
This couples the centering rate to the same neighborhood window used for the EWMA half-life and to the grid spacing of S2.1, so changing $k$ or $r$ keeps the effective dynamics scale-consistent.

\subsection*{S2.3.1 Variational Interpretation and Refinement Admissibility}
\label{sec:si-variational-interpretation}

\paragraph{Latent density and mesh family.}
Let $p$ be the data-generating density on $\mathbb R^d$. The Stage~1 scaffold defines a parametric mixture
\[
q_\theta(\mathbf x)
=\sum_i r_i(\mathbf x;\theta)\,\phi_i(\mathbf x;\theta),
\qquad
\phi_i=\mathcal N(\mathbf w_i,\Sigma_i),
\]
with $r_i$ the routing kernel and $\theta=\{\mathbf w_i,\Sigma_i\}_i$. The routing kernel approximates a Gaussian neighborhood kernel centered at each node: in low ambient dimension ($d\le8$) the implementation uses the Gaussian-relative weights of S2.2 directly, while for higher dimensions a rank-decay approximation preserves the same qualitative shape at lower cost. In either case, $r_i(\mathbf x;\theta)$ is large when $\mathbf x$ is close to $\mathbf w_i$ relative to competing nodes and decays smoothly with distance, so the induced soft assignment $\rho_i(\mathbf x)=r_i/\sum_j r_j$ partitions $\mathbb R^d$ into overlapping \emph{catchments} whose boundaries are determined by the node geometry and the kernel bandwidth (set implicitly by $\tau$ through S2.4). Stage~1 minimizes the forward KL divergence
\[
\mathcal F(\theta)
=\mathrm{KL}(p\,\|\,q_\theta)
=\mathrm{const}-\mathbb E_p[\log q_\theta(\mathbf x)].
\]
Stage~1 does not perform an explicit $\nabla_\theta\mathcal F$ step; rather, the variance-cap projection, EWMA moment tracking, and split/prune dynamics implicitly reduce $\mathcal F$ by keeping each component's catchment-conditional variance within the admissible family.

\paragraph{Catchment-conditional density.}
The catchment mass is $\pi_i=\int\rho_i(\mathbf x)\,p(\mathbf x)\,d\mathbf x$ and the catchment-conditional density is
\[
p_i(\mathbf x)=\frac{\rho_i(\mathbf x)\,p(\mathbf x)}{\pi_i}.
\]
In EWMA equilibrium the per-node statistics recover the catchment-conditional moments:
\[
\mathbf m_i=\mathbb E_{p_i}[\mathbf x-\mathbf w_i],
\qquad
\mathbf s_i=\mathbb E_{p_i}[(\mathbf x-\mathbf w_i)^{\odot 2}],
\qquad
\sigma_i^2=\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf s_i-\mathbf m_i\mathbf m_i^\top).
\]
Thus $\sigma_i^2$ is the trace of the catchment-conditional residual covariance about the current reference $\mathbf w_i$, and $h_i$ is a Monte-Carlo estimator of the catchment mass $\pi_i$ scaled by the total sample count.

\paragraph{Hard-Voronoi variance, soft-routing mean.}
The variance estimator $\sigma_i^2$ and its partition-aligned shadows $(\mathbf s_i^\pm)$ update only on BMU events (rank~0 in the $k$-NN result), so $\sigma_i^2$ measures the hard-Voronoi catchment variance. The residual mean $\mathbf m_i$ (and shadow means $\mathbf m_i^\pm$) update under the full soft routing kernel for all $k$ neighbors. This asymmetry is an instance of the refinement-admissibility principle applied at the routing step: the variance cap is a geometric resolution constraint on each Voronoi cell and should measure only cell-local residuals, while the residual mean is a gradient summary used for centering and Oja direction tracking and benefits from the neighborhood smoothing of the soft kernel. Using the soft kernel for the variance would inflate $\sigma_i^2$ by a $k$- and kernel-dependent factor (residuals from neighboring catchments are large), making the cap comparison dataset-specific rather than geometric.

\paragraph{Variance cap as a resolution constraint.}
The admissible family at scale $\tau$ is
\[
\mathcal Q_\tau
=\bigl\{q_\theta:\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_i)\le\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}\text{ for every }i\bigr\}.
\]
A node with $\sigma_i^2>\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}$ is outside $\mathcal Q_\tau$: its catchment-conditional variance exceeds the cap. Define the \emph{variance surplus}
\[
E_i=(\sigma_i^2-\tau_{\mathrm{local},i})_+\,.
\]
Under the isotropic Gaussian working model $p_i\approx\mathcal N(\mathbf w_i,\sigma_i^2 I_d)$, the per-sample KL deficit between $p_i$ and the best $\tau$-admissible Gaussian $\mathcal N(\mathbf w_i,\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}I_d)$ is
\[
\Delta\mathrm{KL}_i
=\frac{d_{\mathrm{final},i}}{2}
\!\left[\frac{\sigma_i^2}{\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}}
-1
-\log\frac{\sigma_i^2}{\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}}\right]
+O(\|\mathbf m_i\|^2/\tau).
\]
Near the cap ($\sigma_i^2\approx\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}$) the bracketed term is $\tfrac{1}{2}(E_i/\tau_{\mathrm{local},i})^2+O(E_i^3)$, so $E_i$ is, to leading order, the per-sample evidence that the current resolution is insufficient. The accumulated error evidence
\[
\mathcal E_i=h_i\,E_i
\]
is the cumulative log-likelihood deficit for the catchment at scale $\tau$, serving as the continuous-Gaussian analog of the S3.4 BDeu evidence gate.

\paragraph{Splits as variational refinement.}
A split at node $i$ along $\mathbf u_i$ replaces one component with two: $\rho_i\mapsto\rho_{i'}+\rho_k$. The refined family $\mathcal Q'_\tau\supseteq\mathcal Q_\tau$, so $\mathcal F$ weakly decreases.

\paragraph{Refinement-admissibility principle.}
When the variational family is refined (split, recursion-child, Stage~2 entry), each component of node state behaves according to whether it is a sufficient statistic of an invariant marginal or of a partition-conditional density.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Flow-conserved} (sufficient statistics of a partition-marginal that decomposes exactly under refinement): $h_i$, $n_{i\to j}$. These are distributed between the children by the S4.3 flow-conservation rule; their sum is invariant (exactly, under the convention that partition-plane events---measure zero---are routed deterministically; cf.\ S2.3.2).
  \item \emph{Frame-relative} (sufficient statistics conditioned on the catchment partition): $\mathbf m_i$, $\mathbf s_i$, $\sigma_i^2$, $\mathbf a_i$, and the EWMA precision counter (update count). The pre-split aggregate moments are sufficient for the parent catchment $p_i$, but the mapping to the post-split child catchments $p_{i'}$, $p_k$ is non-identifiable from the aggregates alone because the per-sample partition membership was not stored. The prior-consistent baseline is to reset these to the EWMA prior (zero), unless the implementation maintains partition-aligned shadow moments (S2.3.2) that recover identifiability (see S2.3.2 for the streaming workaround).
  \item \emph{Structural / geometric} (parameters of the family itself): $\mathbf w_i$, $\mathbf u_i$, $d_{\mathrm{final},i}$. The parent keeps $\mathbf w_i$; the child inherits $\mathbf u_i$ and $d_{\mathrm{final},i}$.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Connection to cross-frame transfers.}
The three warm-start regimes of S4.4 are instances of this principle: T1 cross-grid applies a partial shrink $\gamma_0\approx0.7$ because the catchment is similar but the scale has changed; T2 parent-to-child applies a full reset because the partition changes drastically; T3 Stage~1-to-Stage~2 applies a partial shrink because the catchment is unchanged but the model class shifts. The S12.6 EWMA noise bound is well-posed precisely because the splitting node and its child enter the post-split trajectory at the EWMA prior (or at the shadow-inherited precision), rather than at the parent's stale over-cap value.

\subsection*{S2.3.2 Partition-Aligned Shadow Moments}
\label{sec:si-shadow-moments}

The moment-reset baseline of S2.3.1 is correct but conservative: it discards evidence that the implementation can retain cheaply. If the node continuously tracks the Oja direction $\mathbf u_i$, a pair of \emph{shadow EWMAs} partitioned by $\mathrm{sign}((\mathbf x-\mathbf w_i)\cdot\mathbf u_i)$ recovers identifiability at the moment of a split.

\paragraph{Shadow state.}
Each node maintains, alongside the aggregate moments $(\mathbf m_i,\mathbf s_i,h_i)$, a positive-side shadow $(\mathbf m_i^+,\mathbf s_i^+,h_i^+)$ and a negative-side shadow $(\mathbf m_i^-,\mathbf s_i^-,h_i^-)$. For a routed sample $\mathbf x$ with residual $\mathbf e_i=\mathbf x-\mathbf w_i$, the aggregate update proceeds as in S2.3, and additionally:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item if $\mathbf e_i\cdot\mathbf u_i>0$, update $(\mathbf m_i^+,\mathbf s_i^+)$ with the same rank-decay-weighted EWMA gain as the aggregate update in S2.3 and residual $\mathbf e_i$, and increment $h_i^+$ by the routing weight;
  \item if $\mathbf e_i\cdot\mathbf u_i<0$, update $(\mathbf m_i^-,\mathbf s_i^-)$ analogously and increment $h_i^-$;
  \item if $\mathbf e_i\cdot\mathbf u_i=0$ (measure-zero event), neither shadow is updated.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Split inheritance.}
When a split fires along $\mathbf u_i$, the positive-side child inherits $(\mathbf m_i^+,\mathbf s_i^+,h_i^+)$ as its initial aggregate moments, and the parent (now the negative-side child) inherits $(\mathbf m_i^-,\mathbf s_i^-,h_i^-)$. Both children reset their own shadow pairs to zero (they will accumulate new shadows against their respective post-split Oja directions). The deferred nudge $\mathbf a_i$ and the EWMA precision counter (update count) reset to zero on both children, because the nudge references the parent's old position basin and the precision counter should reflect the child's local EWMA age.

Hit counts are distributed as $h_{i'}=h_i^-$, $h_k=h_i^+$, satisfying flow conservation $h_{i'}+h_k\le h_i$ (the inequality accounts for any samples that fell exactly on the partition plane). Transition counts on incident links are halved as in S4.3.

\paragraph{Steiner shift at inheritance.}
The shadow moments $(\mathbf m_i^\pm,\mathbf s_i^\pm)$ are accumulated relative to the parent position $\mathbf w_i$, but the children are placed at $\mathbf w_i\pm\delta\,\mathbf u_i$. To reference the inherited moments to the child's new position, a parallel-axis (Steiner) correction is applied: for the positive-side child with displacement $\mathbf d=+\delta\,\mathbf u_i$,
\[
\mathbf m_c=\mathbf m_i^+-\mathbf d,
\qquad
\mathbf s_c=\mathbf s_i^+-2\,\mathbf d\odot\mathbf m_i^++\mathbf d^{\odot 2},
\]
and analogously for the negative-side child with $\mathbf d=-\delta\,\mathbf u_i$. The variance $\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf s_c-\mathbf m_c\odot\mathbf m_c)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf s_i^\pm-\mathbf m_i^\pm\odot\mathbf m_i^\pm)$ is shift-invariant, so the cap-decision state is unchanged; the correction ensures that the post-split residual-mean direction is referenced to the child's actual position rather than the parent's old origin.

\paragraph{Hard-Voronoi variance conservation.}
Under hard Voronoi assignment, the parent cell partitions exactly into the two children's cells at the split plane. The variance evidence is therefore conserved:
\[
\sigma_i^2\,h_i
\;=\;
\sigma_{i'}^{2,+}\,h_i^+\;+\;\sigma_{i'}^{2,-}\,h_i^-.
\]
This holds in the population limit; the EWMA tracks the running approximation within the half-life $k$. Combined with the shadow transfer, the entire variance budget of the parent is distributed to the children without loss or inflation---the children's cap comparisons begin from the exact pre-split evidence rather than from a reset prior.

\paragraph{Identifiability.}
The shadow moments are sufficient statistics for the partition the split commits to, whenever $\mathbf u_i$ is approximately stationary over the shadow accumulation window. They are \emph{not} non-identifiable, because the system chose to maintain them along the axis it will eventually split on. Rapid Oja rotation degrades sufficiency; the staleness reset below addresses this by falling back to the moment-reset baseline of S2.3.1. Subject to this stationarity proviso, shadow transfer is the unique streaming-compatible mechanism for preserving evidence across a refinement when only the dominant partition axis is known in advance.

\paragraph{Oja drift and staleness.}
As $\mathbf u_i$ rotates, the shadow partition plane drifts. The shadow EWMAs use the same half-life $k$ as the aggregate moments, so they track the current Oja direction at the same effective rate. If $\mathbf u_i$ undergoes a large discrete jump (e.g.\ after a nearby topology edit), the shadows can be flagged stale and reset, falling back to the moment-reset baseline of S2.3.1.

\paragraph{Cost.}
Memory: one extra moment pair per node, i.e.\ $3\times$ per-node moment storage; still $O(d)$ and streaming-bounded. Compute: one extra dot product and one branched EWMA update per routed sample per node; no per-sample retention.

\subsection*{S2.3.3 Two-Tier Hebbian Edges: Shadow and Lifted}
\label{sec:si-two-tier-edges}
The scaffold graph is two-tiered, separating raw adjacency evidence from committed topology. For each routed sample the ANN query returns rank-ordered neighbors $\mathcal N_k=(\mathrm{BMU}_1,\mathrm{BMU}_2,\dots)$, and edges update as follows.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Shadow tier (adjacency evidence).} Any routed co-activation between the BMU and a returned neighbor may create or increment a \emph{shadow} edge, accumulating the directed co-activation counters $C(i\!\to\! j)$ at the rank routing weight $\omega_r$ (S2.2). Shadow edges record low-grade adjacency evidence without asserting a topological bond.
  \item \emph{Lifted tier (committed topology).} Only the $\mathrm{BMU}_1\!\to\!\mathrm{BMU}_2$ co-activation---the first- to second-nearest transition---promotes an edge to the \emph{lifted} tier. Lifting is monotone: once lifted, an edge stays lifted until explicitly demoted (S3.1). Both tiers keep the same directed counters; lifting only changes the edge's structural status.
\end{itemize}
The two tiers have distinct consumers:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item the \emph{lifted} graph is the structural graph: it defines the degree-proxy intrinsic dimension $d_{\mathrm{final}}$ (S1.4.1), the flag complex of Stage~2 (S4.1), the connected components and internal/boundary edge sets used by clustering (S2.6.1), the peer-comparison stars for the post-clustering prune pass (S3.1), and the node-count / isolated-node stabilization diagnostics (S2.5.1);
  \item the \emph{full} graph (shadow $\cup$ lifted) supplies the transition evidence read by Affinity Propagation exemplar similarity (S2.6.1), so weak-but-real adjacencies still inform the partition;
  \item link pruning (S3.1) reads \emph{all} edges for the ambient directed-floor pass and the \emph{lifted} edges only for the post-clustering demotion pass; the Dirichlet--multinomial gate (S3.4) scores the directed transition counts on affected stars regardless of tier.
\end{itemize}
This refines the single GNG-style edge convention: ``create/refresh an edge on co-activation'' is the shadow-tier rule, and ``$\mathrm{BMU}_1\!\to\!\mathrm{BMU}_2$ defines topology'' is the lifting rule.

\subsection*{S2.4 Scale Parameter and Control Normalization}
The operational scale variable is the variance cap $\tau$ itself. The controller searches $\tau$ directly on the geometric grid of S2.5, and every downstream quantity ($\delta_{\min}$, $\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}$, and the split cap) reads $\tau$. For interpretability it is sometimes convenient to report a bounded, dimension-normalized progress variable $s_{\mathrm{control}}\in(0,1)$ through the monotone reparameterization
\[
s_{\mathrm{control}}=1-e^{-\tau/D_{\mathrm{subspace}}},
\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
\tau=-D_{\mathrm{subspace}}\log(1-s_{\mathrm{control}}),
\]
where $D_{\mathrm{subspace}}$ is the working dimensionality of the current region's coordinate frame (ambient dimension at the root; the retained PCA rank from S1.4 at child recursion levels). This mapping is a reporting/normalization convention only: $s_{\mathrm{control}}$ has no operational consumer, and no scaffold or controller decision reads it. Within a single scaffold run, all nodes share the same cap: $\tau_{\mathrm{local},i}=\tau$ for every~$i$. Dimensional heterogeneity within a region is resolved by the scale-response machinery (S2.5--S2.6) peaking at different $\tau$ for clusters of different intrinsic dimension, and by recursion giving each cluster its own scale search after PCA transfer. The per-node intrinsic-dimension estimate $d_{\mathrm{final},i}$ is retained as a diagnostic input to junction detection (S8.4) and as the cluster-median aggregation target when a single $d_C$ is required for scale-response normalization, but it does not modulate the split-decision cap. Stationarity at a given $\tau$ is measured by $\mathrm{CV}(\sigma_i^2)$ over mature nodes; since $\tau$ is uniform, equilibrium means uniform-resolution evidence across the scaffold.

\subsection*{S2.5 Scale Response $\Phi_C(\tau)$}
\label{sec:si-scale-response}
For a stabilized scaffold at scale $\tau$, define a per-node Lindeberg-normalized response $R_i(\tau)$ from the node's local hit mass, neighborhood volume proxy, and intrinsic dimension estimate. Let $h_i$ be node $i$'s fractional hit mass, $r_{k,i}$ its distance to the $k$-th nearest scaffold neighbor, $N_C$ the number of routed samples in the current region, $d$ the local working dimension, and $V_d$ the volume of the unit $d$-ball. The standard $k$-NN density proxy is
\[
\widehat\rho_i
=
\frac{h_i\,k\,N_C}{V_d\,r_{k,i}^{\,d}}.
\]
With the scale calibration $\sigma=\sqrt{\tau}/c_{d,k}$ from S11, the Lindeberg-normalized node response is
\[
R_i(\tau)
=
\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{c_{d,k}}\right)^d\widehat\rho_i
=
\left(\frac{\sqrt{\tau}}{c_{d,k}}\right)^d
\frac{h_i\,k\,N_C}{V_d\,r_{k,i}^{\,d}}.
\]
The cluster response is
\[
\Phi_C(\tau)=\sum_{i\in C}R_i(\tau).
\label{eq:si-phi-c}
\]
The sum is the scale-selection object: it detects when the region's support is well resolved at the current variance cap. In an equilibrated cluster, node hit masses are approximately equal, so individual $R_i(\tau)$ values are not reliable within-cluster density indicators. Density and membership later come from the spatial distribution of nodes, per-node residual moments, PL density reconstruction, and Gaussian summaries.

Using the same $r_{k,i}$ values, define the local support proxy
\[
\widehat V_i
=
\frac{V_d\,r_{k,i}^{\,d}}{k/N_C},
\qquad
V_C(\tau)=\sum_{i\in C}\widehat V_i.
\]
The support trace $V_C(\tau)$ is a cheap secondary diagnostic only. The idealized expectation --- that a robust characteristic scale shows a $\Phi_C$ response peak near a plateau transition in $V_C$ (equivalently a knee in the compensated node count $N(\tau)\,\tau^{d/2}$) --- does not hold reliably in practice: node-budget saturation and warm-start path dependence prevent a clean plateau, so the operational selector uses the variance-load up-crossing rather than these traces (S2.5.1, OPEN\_ISSUES~\#28).

\subsection*{S2.5.1 Scale Controller Procedure}
Sweep $\tau$ coarse-to-fine on a geometric grid in $\log\tau$, warm-starting a
single scaffold at the coarsest scale and lowering the cap at each step. A grid
point is eligible only if its Stage~1 scaffold stabilizes under the fixed-scale
criteria; over-coarse grid points that do not stabilize are excluded.

\emph{Operational selector (implemented): variance-load up-crossing.} The
per-scaffold mean load $L(\tau)=\mathbb E[\sigma_i^2/\tau]$ over mature nodes
increases monotonically along the coarse-to-fine grid: at over-coarse scales the
scaffold under-resolves the support and sits below the cap ($L<1$); once the cap
binds, $L>1$. The characteristic scale is the coarsest grid point at which the
mean variance first reaches the cap --- the $L=1$ \emph{up-crossing} --- taken as
whichever endpoint of the straddling grid pair is nearer to $1$. This rule is
scale-invariant: it carries no band constant and no one-step-coarser patch, and
locates $\tau^\star$ within one grid step of the geometric scale on the synthetic
arc/surface suite. If $L$ never reaches $1$ (over-coarse searched band) the finest
stabilized point is taken; if $L\ge 1$ throughout (over-fine band) the coarsest
stabilized point is taken. The reference implementation is
\texttt{stage1.controller.\_select\_load\_crossover}. The legacy load-band
heuristic ($0.65\le L\le1$, coarsest in band) has been removed from the
reference implementation (OPEN\_ISSUES~\#28).

\emph{Support/response traces are diagnostics, not the selector.} The Lindeberg
response $\Phi_C(\tau)$ and the compensated node-count / support trace
$V_C(\tau)\propto N(\tau)\,\tau^{d/2}$ are logged as cross-checks, but neither is
used as the primary operational selector. Empirically (circle, swiss roll; warm-
and cold-started; node budgets $64$--$10^3$) the compensated node-count trace
does \emph{not} exhibit a clean plateau/knee at the characteristic scale: its
log--log slope does not settle at the theoretical $d/2$, and any apparent peak
tracks the node-count budget $N_{\max}$ rather than the geometry. Two effects
confound it --- the finite node budget saturates $N(\tau)$ at fine scales, and
warm-started re-convergence is path-dependent --- so the ``knee in
$N(\tau)\tau^{d/2}$'' proposal is retained only as a diagnostic (see
OPEN\_ISSUES~\#28). The structural counterpart of scale selection --- whether a
region carries genuine multi-cluster substructure at all --- is supplied by
Q-partition \emph{persistence} across adjacent scales
(\hyperref[sec:si-feature-persistence]{S2.6.2}), which is the acceptance-path arbiter;
$L=1$ then fixes the resolution scale of each accepted feature. If no scale
stabilizes with a resolved crossing, the region is terminal for the searched band.

The fixed-$\tau$ stopping rule has a canonical operational default and a
recommended hardening.

\emph{Operational default (implemented).} Stop when the coefficient of variation
of the hard-Voronoi variance over mature nodes stays below the EWMA noise floor
for $M_{\mathrm{eq}}$ consecutive epochs,
\[
\mathrm{CV}(\sigma_i^2)<\beta_{\mathrm{cv}}\sqrt{2/k},
\qquad \beta_{\mathrm{cv}}=1.5,\quad M_{\mathrm{eq}}=3,
\]
whose derivation from the EWMA effective sample size is given in S14.2 (for
$k=8$ the threshold is $\approx0.75$). This is the criterion used by the
reference implementation (\texttt{stage1.stabilization}) and is sufficient for
the synthetic suite.

\emph{Recommended hardening (trend exhaustion).} Where a stronger guarantee is
wanted, augment the absolute threshold with a two-layer confidence test and
treat the threshold itself as a sanity bound rather than the sole signal:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Topology quiet:} over a trailing window of $W$ epochs, there are no
  accepted splits, no accepted node prunes, node count is unchanged, and the
  count of lifted isolated mature nodes is not increasing.
  \item \emph{Moment flatness:} over the same window, the slope of
  $\mathrm{CV}(\sigma_i^2)$ is near zero, its rolling standard deviation is
  small relative to its mean, reconstruction error is no longer trending, and
  the mean load $\mathbb E[\sigma_i^2/\tau]$ is flat rather than still drifting
  toward the cap.
\end{enumerate}
Under this hardening the primary stopping signal is \emph{trend exhaustion}: the
scaffold has become structurally quiet and its moments are no longer evolving
systematically. Optional frozen-topology epochs may be added as a further
diagnostic check. In both regimes the incoherence-based
$\mathrm{CV}(\widetilde\rho)$ criterion remains diagnostic-only (S14.2), because
the incoherence ratio mixes hard-Voronoi $\sigma_i$ with soft-routing
$\mathbf m_i$ and is fragile to neonatal-node transients.

\subsection*{S2.5.2 Local Noise Estimation from Settled Catchments}
\label{sec:si-local-noise}
The same local asymptotic regime that justifies treating a mature catchment as
approximately affine also makes it a probe of the local noise field. Write a
sample near $x_0$ as
\[
X=x_0+Uz+\tfrac12\mathrm{II}(z,z)+\varepsilon,
\]
where $Uz$ is the local tangent contribution, $\mathrm{II}(z,z)$ is the
curvature correction, and $\varepsilon$ is a local noise term with covariance
$\Psi(x_0)$. For a settled single-sheet catchment of effective radius $h$,
\[
\Sigma_{\mathrm{catchment}}
\approx
\Psi(x_0)
+
U\Sigma_z(h)U^\top
+
\Sigma_{\mathrm{curv}}(h)
+
O(h\|\nabla\Psi\|).
\]
Tangential signal variance scales like $O(h^2)$, curvature corrections are
higher order, and floor variation enters through the final term. As $h$
shrinks inside a settled region, the catchment covariance therefore approaches a
patchwise noise tensor plus a controlled geometric term. The practical claim is
not that Proteus recovers a full covariance field everywhere, but that settled
catchments provide the right local statistics from which conservative floor
estimates can be extracted.

This is the bridge between scaffold settlement and local noise estimation:
equilibrium is valuable not only because it stabilizes routing, but because it
produces a region in which residual covariance can be interpreted as local floor
evidence rather than as transient topology noise.

\subsection*{S2.5.3 Minimal Viable Noise Atlas}
\label{sec:si-minimal-noise-atlas}
The publishable baseline is intentionally small. Proteus does \emph{not} require
a full hierarchy of floor models to operationalize local noise. The minimal
atlas consists of three derived quantities:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \textbf{Node-level floor proxy $\psi_i$.} A scalar local residual scale
  derived from settled node moments, e.g.\ a trailing or robust minimum
  component of the residual covariance proxy.
  \item \textbf{Region-level floor summary $u_{\mathrm{local}}$.} A robust region
  aggregate of the node-level proxies, such as a median or trimmed mean over
  mature nodes in the region.
  \item \textbf{Parent-child residual refinement.} At recursion events, refine
  the parent's floor summary by subtracting child-explained covariance from the
  parent's total covariance, yielding a conservative unexplained residual floor.
\end{enumerate}
All three are confidence-gated: low-maturity nodes, unstable catchments, or
disagreeing local summaries reduce confidence and suppress aggressive use of the
floor estimate in controller decisions. This minimal atlas is sufficient to
support local-floor-relative detectability, adaptive scale selection, and
recursion timing without committing the first paper to a full anisotropic
covariance-learning framework.

\subsection*{S2.5.4 Variance-Cap / Heat-Kernel Bridge}
\label{sec:si-tau-heat-bridge}
Stage~1 uses $\tau$ operationally as a hard-Voronoi \emph{variance cap} (S2.4): a
node splits when its catchment variance would exceed $\tau$, so at equilibrium
each mature catchment carries residual variance $\sigma_i^2\lesssim\tau$. The
resolution theory of S2.8 and the two scale-space propositions of the paper
instead treat $\tau$ as the \emph{bandwidth of a Gaussian convolution} acting on
the latent density $\rho$ (the smoothed family $\rho_\tau$ has variance
$\sigma_i^2+\tau$). This subsection states the bridge that lets the same symbol
play both roles and fixes the isotropic convention used by the implementation
and the test harness.

\paragraph{Equilibrium smoothing lemma (informal).}
Fix a region at effective dimension $d$ with a settled scaffold at cap $\tau$,
and let $\widehat\rho^{(C)}_\tau$ be the catchment-conditional density recovered
from the equilibrated nodes (the per-node $k$-NN proxy of S2.5 aggregated over
the region). By the equilibrium calibration of S2.5, a cap-satisfied catchment
locks its effective radius to $r_{k,i}\approx c_{d,k}\sqrt\tau$ (S2.5.5), so estimating the
density from that catchment averages the latent density over a neighborhood of
squared scale $\sim\tau$. Substituting the local expansion of S2.5.2,
$X=x_0+Uz+\tfrac12\mathrm{II}(z,z)+\varepsilon$, gives
\[
\widehat\rho^{(C)}_\tau(x)
=
\bigl(\mathcal G_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{smooth}}}\!*\rho\bigr)(x)
\;+\;
O\!\bigl(\|\mathrm{II}\|^2\,\tau^2\bigr)
\;+\;
O\!\bigl(\tau\,\|\nabla\Psi\|\bigr),
\]
where $\mathcal G_\Sigma$ is the Gaussian kernel with covariance $\Sigma$ and the
error terms are precisely the curvature and floor-gradient corrections of
S2.5.2. The bandwidth is fixed only up to the calibration constant $c_{d,k}$
(S11.1; calibration protocol in S2.5.5), which converts the operational
cap into the effective smoothing scale, $\Sigma_{\mathrm{smooth}}\propto\tau$. In
words: a settled catchment at cap $\tau$ reports the latent density smoothed at
bandwidth $\sim\tau$, up to $c_{d,k}$ and second-order geometry. This is what
licenses S2.8 to reason about $\rho_\tau$ while the controller manipulates the
variance cap directly.

\paragraph{Isotropic convention (canonical for Paper~1).}
The reference implementation and the hierarchy-recovery harness adopt the
isotropic bridge
\[
\Sigma_{\mathrm{smooth}}=\tau\,I,
\]
i.e.\ absorb $c_{d,k}$ into the scale calibration and treat smoothing as
isotropic in the current working frame (ambient at the root, the retained PCA
frame after the T2 transfer of S1.4). This is exact for locally isotropic noise
on flat support and is the convention under which the S2.8 mode-evolution
formulae (variance $\sigma_i^2+\tau$) hold verbatim; it is the declared default,
not a placeholder.

\paragraph{Deferred: anisotropic scale-space.}
An intrinsic/anisotropic bridge --- replacing $\tau I$ by a tangent-space metric
or a covariance semigroup driven by the local noise tensor $\Psi$ (S2.5.2) and
the T2 frame --- would tighten the curvature and floor-gradient error terms above
and is the natural refinement once per-region covariance learning lands. It is
explicitly out of scope for Paper~1.

\subsection*{S2.5.5 Calibration of the Variance-Cap Constant $c_{d,k}$}
\label{sec:si-cdk-calibration}
The scale calibration constant $c_{d,k}$ (S2.5, S2.5.4, S11.1) maps the
operational variance cap $\tau$ to the expected $k$-nearest-neighbor radius of an
equilibrated scaffold under local isotropy,
\[
r_{k,i}\;\approx\;c_{d,k}\,\sqrt{\tau}
\qquad\text{(mature nodes, at equilibrium).}
\label{eq:si-cdk-def}
\]
It converts the hard-Voronoi variance cap into the effective smoothing bandwidth
of S2.5.4 and normalizes the Lindeberg response of S2.5. Because it depends on
the discrete warm-started scaffold dynamics --- not only on the continuum density
--- $c_{d,k}$ is a \emph{calibrated} constant (S14.3): its value is measured on a
declared reference ensemble rather than derived in closed form.

\paragraph{Calibration protocol.}
For each $(d,k)$:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item Draw $n$ points uniformly from the unit $d$-ball (direction uniform on
        $S^{d-1}$, radius $U^{1/d}$, $U\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$).
  \item Run the fixed-$\tau$ Stage~1 scaffold (S2.4--S2.5.1) to equilibrium with
        neighbor count $k$ and cap $\tau_d$ chosen so the equilibrium node count
        lands near a target $N_\star$; using the uniform-density estimate
        $N\approx\bigl(\tfrac{d}{(d+2)\,\tau}\bigr)^{d/2}$ gives
        $\tau_d=\tfrac{d}{d+2}\,N_\star^{-2/d}$. The ratio in
        \eqref{eq:si-cdk-def} is scale-invariant, so the specific $\tau_d$ only
        sets the node budget.
  \item Record the median of $r_{k,i}/\sqrt{\tau_d}$ over mature nodes
        ($\mathrm{update\_count}\ge$ the maturity gate of S3.2); in the
        degenerate case where no node is yet mature, the estimator falls back to
        all nodes.
  \item Repeat over independent ensembles and take the median of the per-scaffold
        medians as $c_{d,k}$.
\end{enumerate}
The reference implementation is
\texttt{proteus.stage1.calibration.calibrate\_cdk}; the shipped lookup table is
\texttt{CDK\_TABLE}, regenerable via
\texttt{python -m proteus.stage1.calibration}.

\paragraph{Isotropic analytic anchor.}
A locally uniform density with mean node spacing $s$ has expected $k$-NN radius
$r_k\approx s\,(k/V_d)^{1/d}$, while the variance cap fixes the catchment radius
through $\tfrac{d}{d+2}R^2=\tau$, i.e.\ $R=\sqrt{(d+2)/d}\,\sqrt\tau$. Taking
$s\propto R$ gives the order-of-magnitude estimate
\[
c_{d,k}^{\mathrm{iso}}
=
\Bigl(\tfrac{k}{V_d}\Bigr)^{1/d}\sqrt{\tfrac{d+2}{d}},
\]
which agrees with the calibrated table to within warm-start, boundary, and
discretization corrections (closest at higher $d$; e.g.\ $d=3,k=8$ gives
$c^{\mathrm{iso}}\approx1.60$ against a calibrated $\approx1.61$). It is used only
as the runtime fallback (\texttt{c\_dk\_analytic}) for dimensions outside the
tabulated grid.

\paragraph{Calibrated values.}
The shipped table, produced on the uniform $d$-ball ensemble with $n=3000$
samples, four ensembles, and target node count $N_\star=120$
(\texttt{CDK\_CALIBRATION\_META}), is
\[
\begin{array}{c|cccc}
c_{d,k} & k{=}6 & k{=}8 & k{=}10 & k{=}12\\
\hline
d{=}1 & 7.94 & 9.71 & 11.22 & 12.67\\
d{=}2 & 2.55 & 2.84 & 3.07 & 3.49\\
d{=}3 & 1.50 & 1.61 & 1.81 & 1.95\\
d{=}4 & 1.28 & 1.32 & 1.38 & 1.46\\
\end{array}
\]
The strong decay in $d$ reflects that in low dimension the $k$-NN radius is large
relative to $\sqrt\tau$. The same uniform-$d$-ball ensemble is the calibration
vehicle for the interior star-radius constant $C_Q(d)$ of S3.3, which measures
the star radius rather than the $k$-NN radius on the identical equilibrated
scaffolds.

\subsection*{S2.6.1 Cluster Identification, Q-Score, and Cross-Scale Tracking}
\label{sec:si-cluster-derivation}
At a fixed variance scale $v$, define the effective correlation radius
\[
\ell(v)=\sqrt{d_{\mathrm{eff}}\,v}.
\]

\paragraph{Scale-conditioned edge evidence.}
The scaffold graph at this scale carries edge evidence combining local geometry
and routed co-activation. On lifted-graph nodes $i,j$ with positions
$\mathbf x_i,\mathbf x_j$, define the Gaussian geometric kernel
\[
K_v(i,j)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lVert\mathbf x_i-\mathbf x_j\rVert^2}
{2\,d_{\mathrm{eff}}\,v}\right),
\]
and, from the directed Hebbian counters $C(i\to j)$ and hit masses $h_i$, the
symmetric co-activation evidence
\[
A_{\mathrm{sym}}(i,j)=\sqrt{\frac{C(i\to j)}{h_i+\varepsilon}\,
\frac{C(j\to i)}{h_j+\varepsilon}}.
\]
The scale-conditioned edge weight is their product,
\[
W_v(i,j)=K_v(i,j)\,A_{\mathrm{sym}}(i,j),
\]
the primitive evidence for every Stage~1 clustering decision: $K_v$ supplies
geometric scale-conditioning (Axiom~2 guarantees it operates on a density
field), while $A_{\mathrm{sym}}$ supplies empirical co-activation evidence from
the scaffold's learning dynamics. A \emph{proposed} extension (OPEN\_ISSUES~\#44;
S2.6.2 hollow-edge) multiplies by a mid-support factor
$M_{\mathrm{transit}}(i,j)\in[0,1]$ measured from data occupancy along the
lifted segment,
\[
W_v(i,j)=K_v(i,j)\,A_{\mathrm{sym}}(i,j)\,M_{\mathrm{transit}}(i,j),
\]
so length-conditioned geometry alone cannot treat a void-bridging shortcut as
strong within-support evidence. Until that factor is wired and calibrated,
$M_{\mathrm{transit}}\equiv 1$ and the product above is unchanged.

\paragraph{Internal, boundary, and pairwise evidence.}
Let $E_v$ be the lifted-edge set at scale $v$. For a candidate node set $S$,
the mean internal evidence and mean boundary evidence are the average
scale-conditioned weights over the internal and boundary lifted edges,
\[
\mathrm{LocalIntra}(S;v)=
\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in E_v,\;i,j\in S}W_v(i,j)}
{|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{in}}(S)|+\varepsilon},
\qquad
\mathrm{BoundaryInter}(S;v)=
\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in E_v,\;i\in S,\;j\notin S}W_v(i,j)}
{|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{bdry}}(S)|+\varepsilon},
\]
where $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{in}}(S)$ and $\mathcal E_{\mathrm{bdry}}(S)$ are the
internal and boundary lifted-edge sets of $S$. For an adjacent pair $S,T$ the
pairwise inter-cluster coupling averages over the crossing lifted edges,
\[
\mathrm{InterLocal}(S,T;v)=
\frac{\sum_{(i,j)\in E_v,\;i\in S,\;j\in T}W_v(i,j)}
{|\mathcal E_{ST}|+\varepsilon}.
\]
The cluster coherence score is then
\[
Q(S;v)
:=
\log\frac{\mathrm{LocalIntra}(S;v)+\varepsilon}
{\mathrm{BoundaryInter}(S;v)+\varepsilon}.
\]
The graph-local cluster definition used in Stage~1 is:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \emph{Coherence:} $Q(S;v)>0$;
  \item \emph{Extent:} $\hat v_S\ge v$, so the set is not sub-resolution at the
  current operating scale;
  \item \emph{Maximality:} no adjacent candidate $T$ has
  $\mathrm{InterLocal}(S,T;v)$ as large as either region's internal evidence.
\end{enumerate}
For a partition $\mathcal P=\{S_1,\dots,S_k\}$, the region-weighted quality is
\[
Q(\mathcal P;v)=\sum_{j=1}^k\frac{|S_j|}{n}Q(S_j;v).
\]
Merges are admissible only when the merged partition increases
$Q(\mathcal P;v)$ and the inter-cluster coupling is no weaker than the weaker
of the two intra-cluster coherences; split proposals are admissible only when
every child still satisfies coherence and extent at the current scale.

At the selected scale $\tau_C^\star$, Stage~1 uses Affinity Propagation on the
stabilized Hebbian graph to produce candidate exemplars and fragments.
Similarities are computed only on existing edges of the full graph (shadow
$\cup$ lifted; S2.3.3) using smoothed transition probabilities and a
smoothed-PMI score. For directed Hebbian counter $C(i\to j)$, define
\[
\widehat q(j\mid i)
=
\frac{\alpha_{0,i}+C(i\to j)}
{\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal N(i)}(\alpha_{0,i}+C(i\to\ell))}.
\]
Let $K_i=|\mathcal N(i)|$ and define the smoothed marginal node mass
\[
\widehat P(i)
=
\frac{h_i+\alpha_{0,i}K_i}
{\sum_v(h_v+\alpha_{0,v}K_v)}.
\]
For an undirected Hebbian edge $\{i,j\}$, use the symmetrized joint estimate
\[
\widehat P(i,j)
=
\frac12\left(\widehat P(i)\widehat q(j\mid i)
+\widehat P(j)\widehat q(i\mid j)\right),
\]
and set the AP similarity to smoothed PMI,
\[
s(i,j)
=
\log\widehat P(i,j)-\log\widehat P(i)-\log\widehat P(j).
\]
No similarity is assigned to unlinked node pairs. The AP self-preference is derived from the same BDeu concentration:
\[
s(i,i)=-2\log(d_{\mathrm{final},i}+1).
\]
This makes low-dimensional regions more willing to instantiate exemplars and high-dimensional regions more conservative.

AP exemplars are discrete representatives for graph clustering; they are not the continuous modes used in fuzzy membership. The directional information discarded by symmetrization is retained as a diagnostic
\[
A(i,j)
=
\frac{C(i\to j)-C(j\to i)}
{C(i\to j)+C(j\to i)+\varepsilon}.
\]
$A(i,j)$ is not used for AP clustering; it is logged for dimensionality-junction diagnostics. Across adjacent grid values, clusters can be tracked by node-ID overlap because the scaffold is warm-started. After selection, each AP cluster becomes a child region, and samples route to the child owning their BMU.

AP fragments are only proposals. Stage~1 subsequently performs Q-gated pair
merges, boundary refinement, and residual cleanup so that the accepted
partition satisfies the graph-local cluster definition rather than merely the AP
objective.

\paragraph{Scope of the single-scale criterion.}
The graph-local score $Q(S;v)$ is a \emph{proposal-level} screen: it certifies
that a candidate set is internally more coherent than its boundary is leaky at
the single operating scale $v$. It is \emph{not}, on its own, a sufficient
acceptance test for how many clusters a connected region contains, for two
structural reasons. First, the region-weighted partition score
$Q(\mathcal P;v)$ is degenerate against the single-cluster null: a whole
connected component has an empty boundary edge set, so
$\mathrm{BoundaryInter}=0$ and $Q\to+\infty$; the one-cluster ``partition''
therefore dominates $Q(\mathcal P;v)$ trivially, and a same-criterion
comparison against it cannot arbitrate a proposed split. Second, and more
fundamentally, a uniform manifold sampled coarsely (e.g.\ a ring resolved into a
handful of arcs) and a genuinely multi-modal region can present
\emph{indistinguishable} single-scale statistics: their per-cluster $Q$,
$\mathrm{InterLocal}/\mathrm{LocalIntra}$ ratios, extent ratios
$\hat v_S/v$, conductances, and modularities overlap, because the distinction
between an intrinsic feature of $\rho$ and a composite summary of unresolved
children is a statement about behaviour \emph{across} scales, not at one. The
canonical arbiter of this ambiguity is therefore feature persistence
(S2.6.2): a multi-cluster partition is accepted only when the same coarse
partition persists over an interval of adjacent scales, whereas the transient
arc-partition of a uniform manifold does not. When the Stage~2 machinery is
available, the Dirichlet--multinomial evidence gate (\hyperref[sec:si-dm-evidence]{S3.4})
supplies the complementary likelihood-ratio null that $Q(\mathcal P;v)$ cannot.
Accordingly, the boundary-refinement and residual-cleanup passes referenced
above are \emph{operational stand-ins} for the persistence signal: they
approximate ``merge fragments that would not persist as independent features''
using single-scale side information (component structure, size balance). They
are intended for eventual replacement by the persistence criterion (S2.6.2) or
the Stage~2 Dirichlet--multinomial evidence gate
(\hyperref[sec:si-dm-evidence]{S3.4}). Empirically, however, they remain
\emph{load-bearing}: the persistence rule as currently operationalized does not
yet dominate them (see the warm-start failure mode in S2.6.2), so they are
retained as the operating acceptance path until that rule is hardened. They are
not canonical single-scale constants and carry no derivation status.

\subsection*{S2.6.2 Feature Persistence and Recursion Timing}
\label{sec:si-feature-persistence}
Every scale-space feature has a persistence interval: the range of scales over
which it satisfies the cluster definition. There are two distinct cases.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \textbf{Intrinsic features:} true features of the unsmoothed density
  $\rho$ itself. They have a coarse-end death scale
  $v_{\mathrm{death}}^+$ above which smoothing destroys their contrast or merges
  them into a neighbor. By Gaussian non-enhancement they persist monotonically
  toward finer scales.
  \item \textbf{Composite features:} features of the smoothed family $\rho_v$
  that summarize unresolved children over an intermediate range of scales. They
  have both a coarse-end death scale $v_{\mathrm{death}}^+$ and a fine-end death
  scale $v_{\mathrm{death}}^-$ below which they resolve into children.
\end{itemize}

For a feature $F_k$, the useful characteristic scales are
$v_{\mathrm{death},k}^+$, $v_k^\ast$, $\sigma_k^2$,
$v_{\mathrm{death},k}^-$, and $\tau_{\mathrm{sep},kj}$. Here $v_k^\ast$ is the
preferred operating scale, $\sigma_k^2$ is the intrinsic width,
$v_{\mathrm{death},k}^-$ is defined only for composites, and
$\tau_{\mathrm{sep},kj}$ is the scale at which a feature first becomes
distinguishable from neighbor $j$. Traversing from coarse to fine, a
well-posed feature satisfies
\[
v_{\mathrm{death},k}^+ > v_k^\ast \gtrsim \sigma_k^2 > v_{\mathrm{death},k}^-.
\]
For intrinsic features, $v_{\mathrm{death},k}^-=0$.

This yields the recursion-timing interpretation used by Stage~1. The discovery
objective is coarse-to-fine: identify a feature as close to $v_k^\ast$ as
possible without overshooting its coarse-end death. For composites, parent
fine-end death and child coarse-end birth are dual signals of the same event.
In the clean handoff regime,
\[
v_{\mathrm{death},\mathrm{parent}}^-
\approx
\max_j v_{\mathrm{death},\mathrm{child}_j}^+.
\]
The parent begins to fail coherence or maximality at roughly the same scale at
which its strongest child becomes independently detectable. Monitoring both
signals makes recursion predictive rather than purely reactive.

\paragraph{Operational persistence signal.}
The scale controller (S2.5.1) already sweeps $\tau$ coarse-to-fine on the
geometric grid. The persistence criterion is evaluated on this sweep by
recording the accepted partition $\mathcal P(\tau_j)$ at each grid point and
comparing adjacent partitions. Partitions are compared in \emph{sample space}:
each accepted clustering induces a labeling of the region's samples, and samples
retain a stable identity across grid points even though the scaffold's node set
changes under splits and prunes. Two adjacent partitions are judged ``the same''
when their optimally matched clusters (maximum-intersection assignment) have mean
Jaccard overlap at least $\theta_{\mathrm{ovl}}$, averaged over
$\max(K_j,K_{j+1})$ slots so that a change in cluster count is penalized. A
multi-cluster partition is accepted only if it persists over at least
$P_{\mathrm{persist}}=2$ adjacent grid points --- the minimal non-trivial
interval consistent with the persistence definition above. This minimality fixes
the \emph{smallest admissible} interval; it is an operational default, not a
derived guarantee that a two-point agreement is \emph{sufficient} evidence of an
intrinsic feature (see the warm-start failure mode below). The characteristic
scale of an accepted feature is the coarsest grid point of its persistence
interval (its coarse-end death $v_{\mathrm{death}}^+$); the threshold
$\theta_{\mathrm{ovl}}$ only fixes partition identity. Under a sufficiently
robust acceptance rule, a uniform manifold sampled into transient arcs should
fail this test---its arc-partition does not recur coherently across scales---
whereas a genuinely multi-modal region persists from its coarse-end death over a
long, high-overlap run, which is precisely the discrimination the single-scale
$Q$ of S2.6.1 cannot make.

\paragraph{Optional within-interval resolution (hybrid; OPEN\_ISSUES~\#28).}
When \texttt{ScaleSearchConfig.selector}=\texttt{"persistence"}, the structural
accept/reject decision is the coarse-anchored persistence interval above, and by
default $\tau^\star$ lands at that interval's coarse end
(\texttt{PersistenceConfig.resolve\_within\_interval}=\texttt{"none"}).
The optional hybrid mode \texttt{"load\_crossover"} keeps persistence as the
arbiter but re-picks $\tau^\star$ by running the S2.5.1 variance-load $L{=}1$
up-crossing on the accepted persistent \emph{subgrid} only. Empirically
(hierarchy), within-block loads sit above~$1$, so the hybrid still returns the
coarsest stabilized point of the block ($\tau^\star\approx0.2$, roughly $9\times$--$16\times$
the fine-leaf packing scale that unit tests call \texttt{expected\_tau}) --- a
category mismatch, not a failed load-crossover. Two additional
\emph{experimental} landings (default off; proposal-path probes) keep
persistence as arbiter and re-pick $\tau^\star$ inside the accepted persistent
grid block:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
  \item \texttt{"mid\_interval"} --- midpoint of the accepted block (A6-T28 /
  A6-T32); hierarchy Phi probe $\approx 2.7\times$ fine-leaf
  \texttt{expected\_tau}.
  \item \texttt{"mid\_interval\_load\_screened"} --- same mid landing, but
  \emph{reject} to the coarse-end $i_{\mathrm{lo}}$ when the variance load at
  the candidate is $\ll 1$ (shared unit floor
  $\texttt{\_WITHIN\_INTERVAL\_LOAD\_SCREEN\_MIN}{=}0.5$; A6-T40). On hierarchy
  the mid load is $\gg 1$, so the screen does \emph{not} fire and
  screened${=}$raw mid --- the $2.7\times$ overshoot is \emph{not} a low-load
  artifact. Default remains \texttt{"none"}.
  \item \texttt{"two\_thirds\_interval"} --- two-thirds of the way from coarse
  to fine within the accepted block
  ($i_{\mathrm{lo}}+2(i_{\mathrm{hi}}-i_{\mathrm{lo}})\!/\!3$; A6-T43..T45);
  hierarchy Phi probe $\approx 1.49\times$ \texttt{expected\_tau} --- brackets
  mid overshoot and three-quarter undershoot on the discrete grid, but
  three-quarter remains closer to $E[\tau]$. Default remains \texttt{"none"}.
  \item \texttt{"two\_thirds\_load\_screened"} --- same two-thirds landing under
  the \emph{same} shared $\ll 1$ floor
  ($\texttt{\_WITHIN\_INTERVAL\_LOAD\_SCREEN\_MIN}{=}0.5$; A6-T43). On hierarchy
  the two-thirds load is $\gg 1$, so the screen does \emph{not} fire and
  screened${=}$raw --- the $1.49\times$ overshoot is \emph{not} a low-load
  artifact. Default remains \texttt{"none"}.
  \item \texttt{"three\_quarter\_interval"} --- three-quarters of the way from
  coarse to fine within the accepted block
  ($i_{\mathrm{lo}}+3(i_{\mathrm{hi}}-i_{\mathrm{lo}})\!/\!4$; A6-T34..T36);
  hierarchy Phi probe $\approx 0.82\times$ \texttt{expected\_tau} --- closest
  of the experimental landings to $E[\tau]$ but a slight undershoot. Do not
  promote to default on this alone.
  \item \texttt{"three\_quarter\_load\_screened"} --- same three-quarter
  landing under the \emph{same} shared $\ll 1$ floor
  ($\texttt{\_WITHIN\_INTERVAL\_LOAD\_SCREEN\_MIN}{=}0.5$; alias
  $\texttt{\_THREE\_QUARTER\_LOAD\_SCREEN\_MIN}$; A6-T38/T40). On hierarchy
  the three-quarter load is $\gg 1$ ($\sim 19$), so the screen does
  \emph{not} fire and screened${=}$raw --- the $0.82\times$ undershoot is
  therefore \emph{not} a low-load artifact. Default remains \texttt{"none"}.
  \item \texttt{"fine\_end\_of\_block"} --- finest grid point of the accepted
  block (A6-T31); hierarchy Phi probe \emph{undershoots}
  \texttt{expected\_tau} ($\approx 0.25\times$), so treat as an overshoot-risk
  diagnostic rather than a candidate default.
  \item \texttt{"load\_weighted\_interval"} --- among indices in the accepted
  block with load $\ge\texttt{\_WITHIN\_INTERVAL\_LOAD\_SCREEN\_MIN}$, pick
  $\arg\min_i|\log L_i|$ (closest-to-unit load; A6-T47); fall back to
  $i_{\mathrm{lo}}$ when none clear the screen. Distinct from the
  load-crossover \emph{straddle} hybrid. On hierarchy (seed~$0$) the pick is
  the coarse end ($L\sim 0.73$ at $i_{\mathrm{lo}}$; $\tau^\star/E[\tau]
  \sim 16\times$) --- not a fine-leaf recovery. Multi-seed lock (A6-T49 /
  A3-T55): across hierarchy seeds $0$--$4$ (std and densified grids),
  load-weighted systematically reproduces the \texttt{"none"} / coarse-end
  landing whenever a multi-cluster interval is accepted --- within-block loads
  jump from $L(i_{\mathrm{lo}})\sim 0.6$--$0.7$ to $L\gg 1$ at the next grid
  point, so $\arg\min|\log L|$ among $L\ge 0.5$ is always $i_{\mathrm{lo}}$.
  Seeds $1$--$2$ never accept a persistent split (modes agree via LC
  fallback). Load-weighted is therefore \emph{not} a useful within-interval
  refinement on this family. Default remains \texttt{"none"}.
\end{itemize}
Ordering on the hierarchy Phi table (seed~$0$; A6-T41/T44; standard geometric
grid $r{=}1/\sqrt{2}$, $\le 12$ pts): mid
$\sim 2.69\times$ (raw${=}$load-screened) $\ge$ two-thirds $\sim 1.49\times$
(raw${=}$load-screened) $\ge$ three-quarter $\sim 0.82\times$
(raw${=}$load-screened; closest) $\ge$ fine-end $\sim 0.25\times$;
load-crossover / \texttt{"none"} / load-weighted remain $\sim 16\times$.
Densifying the grid with experimental
\texttt{ScaleSearchConfig.halve\_grid\_steps} (half log-step
$r\!\mapsto\!\sqrt{r}$, up to $2\times\max\_grid\_points$; A6-T46) \emph{flips}
the fractional ranking on the same scene: two-thirds $\sim 1.00\times$ while
three-quarter undershoots further ($\sim 0.76\times$) --- evidence that
standard-grid ``3q closest'' is discrete-grid quantization, not an intrinsic
preference for the $3/4$ fraction (A6-T46..T48 / paper). That densify flip is
\emph{seed-fragile} (A6-T49 / A3-T55): seed~$4$ matches seed~$0$ on the
standard grid (3q closer) but under \texttt{halve\_grid\_steps} persistence
rejects ($\tau^\star$ index \texttt{None}), so all fractional modes fall back
to the same LC coarse landing --- densify fragility, not a preserved ranking
flip. Multi-seed Phi hierarchy export (A6-T50 / A3-EXP-si63;
\texttt{test\_export\_multi\_seed\_phi\_hierarchy\_table}): seeds $0$--$4$
$\times$ std/dense grids lock persist-accept $\{0,3\}$ on both grids and
seed~$4$ on the standard grid only (densified seed~$4$ persist-reject; all
modes share the LC fallback). Seed-$0$ std ratios pin mid$\sim 2.69$ /
$2/3\sim 1.49$ / $3q\sim 0.82$ / fine-end$\sim 0.25$; densify flips
$2/3\sim 1.00$ vs $3q\sim 0.76$. Seed-$3$ std short persistence block
(A6-T55 / A6-T59 / A3-EXP-si67): accepted $\mathrm{run}_0$ length${=}3$
forces mid$\equiv 2/3\equiv 3q$ at index~$1$ ($\tau^\star/E\approx 8.83\times$)
via integer floor; densify expands $\mathrm{run}_0{=}16$ and restores
fractional separation$+$densify flip. Seed-$4$ Jaccard / run-length
export (A6-T56 / A3-EXP-si67): standard-grid all adjacent overlaps
agree; densify first half-step $\mathrm{ov}_0\approx 0.39$ breaks
$\mathrm{run}_0{=}1$ while fineward $\mathrm{run}_1\ge P_{\mathrm{persist}}$
is discarded by coarse-anchoring. Load-weighted
$\times$ \texttt{halve\_grid\_steps} combo (A6-T51) never refines past
coarse-end / LC on persist-accept seeds $0/3/4$. Circle and swiss-roll
hybrids / experimental modes (including load-screened mid / two-thirds /
three-quarter / load-weighted) are identical no-ops --- no persistent
split, so ordinary $L{=}1$ fallback already near geometric truth
(A6-T42/T45 / paper); under \texttt{halve\_grid\_steps} the same
circle/swiss LC identity holds (persist-reject; densify moves the LC peak
only; A6-T53 / A3-T61), and circle densify$\times$load-weighted $\equiv$
